ParlaMint-ES_2020-06-10-CD200610.u226	Thank you very much, Mr. Matute. [[A cleaning service worker disinfects the speaker's rostrum]] Thank you very much, Mrs. Guille. For the Basque Parliamentary Group, Mr Barandiaran Benito has the floor. [[EAJ-PNV]]
ParlaMint-ES_2020-06-10-CD200610.u227	Thank you very much, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, Mr Vice-President, Mr Minister, I hope you will join us. Probably the real decree-law that we are going to evaluate today and I hope that to validate will be the most important social policy measure that has been taken in recent history. It is - as you have recalled - anchored in the Constitution, first in article 9.2, in that it urges the public authorities to promote the conditions for the freedom and equality of the individual and of the groups in which they are integrated to be effective, and, as you commented, equality is a parameter that is also handled through this royal decree-law. In addition, it is set out in Article 41, which states that the public authorities will maintain a public social security system for all citizens guaranteeing adequate social assistance and benefits in the face of situations of need, especially in the event of unemployment. And that is the minimum living income, which - according to these two constitutional parameters - is promoted through this royal decree-law as a non-contributory benefit of the social security system. This enables measures that are redistributive of income to be adopted as a tool for the eradication of extreme poverty and social inclusion. Thus, the object of the minimum income is to guarantee a decent income for living units in a situation of social vulnerability, as well as to establish a mesh of basic and general equity, which can be complemented with other benefits and income in each territory according to its own realities. The Constitutional Court, in its judgement 239/2002, states that this social security protection action is a function of the State to meet needs that may go beyond the contributory coverage of the system itself and therefore may include both contributory and assistance benefits in its scope. Article 41, as also recalled by the TC, establishes the duty of the public authorities to maintain a certain protective structure of citizens in the face of situations of need and sets out lines that must guide the evolution of that one, but it is not a suitable precept to assign competences nor to decide in favor of some or other decision-making centers among those who integrate the model of articulation of the State. This is why Article 41 is a neutral provision which imposes commitments, to which reference has been made, on the public authorities, but it does not prejudge which of them must assume those obligations. Well, it is in this context of constitutional regulation and attention to needs and redistribution, which enshrines the agreement signed by my group, the Basque Nationalist Group, and the Government of Spain on minimum living income, because it demanded scrupulously and correctly resolving the link between the new benefit and those already existing in the Autonomous Community of the Basque Country and the Navarre Forum. In this respect, the three premises on which we would have started at the time of the negotiation have been fulfilled: complementarity, compatibility and subsidiarity between the two services, between the two protection systems. The minimum living income is likely to be supplemented as such by the minimum guarantee for benefits and aid, if any, established by the Autonomous Communities and other public administrations. The Euskadi GIR and the guaranteed income in Nafarroa, to which reference has been made, as social assistance benefits are subsidiary and, where appropriate, complementary to others to which the beneficiary may be entitled, which supports an order of priority between the two in the sense that access to and the amount of minimum living income is not affected by the perception of others such as GIR or guaranteed income or similar income from other autonomous communities, the latter being also compatible with the previous one. But it is also a singular fact - the singularity also characterizes this new benefit - that the minimum vital income is defined as a non-contributory economic benefit of the Social Security whose management is the responsibility of the INSS, which does not prevent such management from being assumed by the Autonomous Community of the Basque Country or by the Foral Community, as is currently done with the non-contributory pensions of the Social Security, and can also be included, in the case of the Autonomous Community of the Basque Country, in the claimed fulfilment of the provision of the statute in its article 18. In this connection, I was encouraged that Mr Escriva himself, in his presentation to the Committee on Labour, Inclusion, Social Security and Migration a couple of days ago, understood and defined this issue as a matter of efficiency in management, since, while noting the effectiveness of anti-poverty models and the inclusion of the Basque Community and of Navarre, warned that one of the essential requirements for defining the conditions for access to minimum income is the finding, the contrast between the level of income and wealth, which in the case of the Basque country and Navarre means maintaining that competence at the headquarters of the outsourced entities which hold them in the field of finance. It would have been a misconception not to have placed this benefit in institutions whose management of a similar benefit has been proven effective in the fight against poverty and social exclusion, and with greater reason if it requires tax data that hold outland finances. Thus, citizens have effective administrations that can test compliance with the requirements required to be beneficiaries of the benefit, as well as currently have a very deep knowledge of this type of aid, which will serve to connect the GIR, income guarantee income, with the new minimum living income. In this way, people who receive GIR and guaranteed income will not be affected by their current situation, nor will those who are entitled to minimum living income or the rest of the groups who are not covered by that minimum living income today, but are in GIR, as is the case with pensioners and persons without legal residence. All of them will continue to receive the amounts currently received and, where appropriate, higher for persons receiving GIR and guarantee income, which is constituted by a complementary network of dignity. The reality is that Euskadi continues to invest to guarantee income to the most disadvantaged groups. It thus maintains a necessary connection for its complementarity by linking it with the common objective. But all this requires the State to commit itself also to the transfer of the management of the minimum income without delay, as stipulated in the fifth additional provision of the law. This also implies the effectiveness of Article 18(2)(b) of the Statute of Autonomy in accordance with Judgment 133/2019. Well, once this transfer takes place, the autonomous community will take charge of the full financing of the minimum living income in its territory and the State will be able to stop financing this non-contributory benefit, which would become a burden borne by the Basque Country. The vital minimum income - I am already finishing, that time is running out -, ladies and gentlemen, represents in the State what the GIR has represented since 1990 in the Autonomous Community of the Basque Country or what has meant since 1999 the guaranteed income in Navarre. It is a wealth-sharing effort that Euskadi has been making for more than thirty years, and that probably has much to do with poverty rates being lower than those of the state, even lower than those of European countries that we consider to be more advanced. Eurostat data for 2018 confirm that the incidence of poverty in Euskadi is 18%, lower than that of the state as a whole, which is 26%, even lower than the European average of 22%. Let us hope that these are also the achievements of minimum living income. That is our wish and that is why we will support this royal decree-law. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES_2020-06-10-CD200610.u239	Thank you, President. Vice-President, minister, today with the minimum vital income - already mentioned here - a historic measure is taken that allows Spain to advance in social justice. Minimum vital income is a fundamental measure to end poverty; the United Nations rapporteur, who visited us not long ago and saw the situation of exclusion in which many people were in this country, told us; he has therefore very positively appreciated this measure. The minimum living income is a safety net for all people throughout this country and is going to be an income floor for all families. From now on, citizenship in Spain will be more protected, it can enjoy more security in lives full of uncertainties, of uncertainties caused by the vital precariousness as a result of neoliberal policies of the right. Faced with the release of 2008, a key exit of rights cuts, this government turns 180 degrees and bets to recover rights, yes, but it also expands the social rights catalogue and this is important: minimum living income is a new social right. Moreover, it is a right that I would say opens the door to a new generation of rights where important changes are already perceived. This right is no longer directly linked to work and the labour market, as the minister said, because we are no longer in a Keynesian society of full employment, because work, unfortunately, is no longer the method of social integration. Thus, the new social rights cannot be linked exclusively to the worker, as was done at the birth of the social State - and I speak in masculine terms to the worker - but the new social rights must revolve around the person and his dignity. The feminists have long been betting on the de-laboration of social rights, and in this sense the minimum living income is also revolutionary, because it points to a necessary update of our welfare state, because it puts at the center the lives of people, of all people, regardless of their relationship to the labour market. Now I'd like to do a little democratic pedagogy, especially for the right wing. When some call in a derogatory way the minimum income vital 'paguita', showing - I would say - a great ignorance, I would tell you a couple of things. With the minimum vital income the democratic state - our democratic state - becomes bigger and stronger because there will be more freedom and more equality, which, as you know, are the foundations of any democratic state. If we talk about freedom, the Spanish Constitution recognizes it in article 1.1, but it not only recognizes formal freedom, freedom of non-interference, but also positive freedom. In order for individuals to truly enjoy freedom, they must have a minimum of economic security. If they do not have minimum material resources, that freedom is fictitious, it is a hollow word. And it's not something new; it's not something new. Eighty years ago, Franklin Roosevelt, in a famous speech in Congress, spoke of the four freedoms: freedom of expression, freedom of worship, freedom not to be afraid and freedom to live without hardship, freedom not to be in need. This way of talking about freedoms already involved openness to social rights, especially the right to an adequate standard of living, and had great international influence and was reflected, as we know, in the Universal Declaration of Human Rights. So, in short, there is no democracy without freedom and there is no real freedom when the refrigerator cannot be filled. The second of the links of this vital minimum income with the democratic State is equality. Our Constitution recognizes equality, but not only formal equality, not only non-discrimination, but also material equality. Article 9.2 gives a mandate to the public authorities to achieve equality under the real conditions of existence. Therefore, that maximum of 'we are all equal before the law' is neither true nor sufficient, first, because we are not all - because we are all and because we are 'all' - nor are we equal, because we belong to different families and are born under different economic conditions. Equality of opportunity will therefore once again depend on the guarantee of these minimum living conditions and minimum living income is a key element in achieving that goal, in advancing on an equal footing. In addition, he would add something else: minimum living income was already at the heart of the constitutional system itself. It is something that is there and that is derived directly from some precepts that we all know: from the declaration of the social State, from the right to life and from the dignity of the person. All of this makes us all entitled to existence, that is to say to a minimum living income. And it took this government to get it on paper and to get it to all the citizens. Really, I congratulate you. It is something that makes the democratic state and our constitutional system bigger. I'm finishing up. It is true that minimum living income has as its main function to end or combat the extreme poverty of our country, but perhaps we must not forget about everything I have said that what is going to make minimum living income is to strengthen our Constitution, the social and democratic constitutionalism of this country. So I end by saying: congratulations, vice president, congratulations, minister, because at a very hard and very difficult time that we have all lived have been able to expand democracy in the Spanish state, and this does not happen every day. Thank you. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-06-10-CD200610.u268	Thank you, Madam President. [[applause]] For this reason, ladies and gentlemen, the Socialist Government of Zapatero in 2007 passed the Historical Memory Act. Because our country had the right to know the truth of what the military coup of Franco and Francoism meant, and our democracy had to do justice and repair the victims of the Civil War and the Franco dictatorship. Because a full and healthy democracy, ladies and gentlemen, could not consent to having in the square of Santa Ana the sculpture of Federico García Lorca, while in the square of San Juan de la Cruz was Franco on horseback. Because a healthy democracy, a full democracy, could not bear to have in its street streets dedicated to Millán-Astray while next door there was a square dedicated to Antonio Machado. That is why the law of democratic memory, the law of historical memory, and there is still a way to go, as Antonio Machado said. That is why my group, together with the Group of the European People's Party, is bringing this initiative to plenary, because there is still a way to go, ladies and gentlemen; a way to go in the recovery of the democratic memory of our country. One of these paths is to prevent institutions of our State, that the already democratic institutions of our State can reward people who, by relying precisely on those institutions, act against their values. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we must seek the legal way to revoke the decorations and rewards granted by the State to officials or public authorities who, before or after that concession, perform acts or conduct incompatible with democratic values and with the principles of human rights protection. Ladies and gentlemen, as you know, the intention of the Socialist Government and the current coalition Government to withdraw these decorations is not new. In fact, the former socialist government has already begun to work on this path. It was the socialist government, with Minister Marlaska, in June 2018 that demanded a report to know the decorations that Billy the Child had received during his police career. In October 2018 Minister Marlaska announced a normative text to proceed with the withdrawal of these medals to Billy the Child, a text that was ready, that only needed a legal framework to include it, but, as you well know, there was an electoral advance that stopped this from happening. The commitment of the Socialist Group and the Socialist Party remained intact, and this was reflected in our April 2019 electoral programme, in which we talked not only about police officers, but about anyone - whether official or not - who had received any official decoration and acted in a manner incompatible with human rights and democratic values. Ladies and gentlemen, the coalition government, the current government between PSOE and the United Nations Podemos, included in its agreement precisely this issue that we are bringing to plenary today. We bring it to revalidate our commitment, to revalidate the commitment, even after the death of Billy the Child, to regulate the withdrawal of these decorations and to move further forward, ladies and gentlemen; not only to withdraw these decorations, but to go further in an important reform of the Historical Memory Act of 2007 that leads us to a new law, to a law of democratic memory. The law of 2007 was a milestone in the development of our country as a democratic country and which was watching over public policies of memory. However, with more than a decade of perspective we can see the need to update that law, which, as I say, has been in force for ten years and in which we want to go further in order to achieve the objectives already set by that same law. To advance with a new law of democratic memory that allows exhuming the victims who are still buried in the graves, and to do so with the support of the public, the State, with the funding of the State, supporting families so that they can recover the remains of their family members, with a DNA bank that allows to identify victims and family members. To protect also through this law of democratic memory those significant spaces and places during the Civil War and the Franco dictatorship, and to be able to fix a day in the year to remember and recognize all the victims of the Franco dictatorship, because, ladies and gentlemen, forgetfulness is a condemnation that our democracy cannot apply to those who gave their lives for a Spain in freedom. And look, ladies and gentlemen, surely this is a difficult issue, we have already found it difficult to withdraw these decorations, but it was harder to get Franco out of the Valley of the Fallen and we did. [[Applauds]] We'll do this too. Ladies and gentlemen, today is a memorable day for our democracy, today we are adopting a new right, minimum living income, and soon there will be a new right, the right that our democracy deserves to the dignity, justice and reparation of the victims of Francoism. Nothing more and thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-06-10-CD200610.u284	Thank you very much, President. Fernando Bárcenas collects in his work Learning from pain after Auschwitz important reflections of German thinkers on the meaning of oblivion and memory, in relation to dark passages and times, such as that of Nazism in Germany. Hans Küng, the German theologian, points out that in the face of a gloomy past every people, without exception, prefers the art of forgetting that of remembering. But this forgetfulness of the past does not cease to be another manifestation of the power of that gloomy past over the present, facilitating conditions for, if necessary, reincarnation in the future. For this reason, the bet on memory, in the face of oblivion, seeks to remember, so as not to repeat, a tragic past. The bet on memory is, in short and above all, an ethical responsibility that the dead demand from the living, so that we can integrate within our consciousness their own lasting footprint, in the form of an ethics. However, Hans Küng also continues to point out to us, through the work he quoted, that the purpose of memory cannot be instrumentalized as a means of creating identity, for the interests of party or state ideology, but rather in attention to the ethical differentiation between humanity and inhumanity, and the ethical differentiation between good and evil, in order to achieve a critical relationship with history itself, which will enable us to achieve a deeper moral awareness and greater human sensitivity, and which will encourage us not to repeat the mistakes of the past; errors that can undoubtedly be repeated if we do not erect strong moral, legal and political dikes on the value and respect for the different and the different. Perhaps in this society that we live in has not yet reached this point of finding a definitive and shared soil on memory, as learning from that differentiation of humanity and inhumanity, good and evil, and as a profound reconciliation; although it is also true that, slowly and in an attempt, the cloak of silence and forgetfulness is rising on our tragic past. A first legal milestone was the same Constitution of 78, when it advocates freedom, justice, pluralism and democratic coexistence as superior values of its legal system, among others, by establishing itself as a social and democratic State governed by law. It was followed by various measures and rights, which were recognized until the adoption of Law 52/07, known as the Historical Memory Act, which our parliamentary group supported, and which is the time to update its content and ensure that its implementation is not as porous as it has been. This updating of content will have the opportunity to discuss it in connection with the procedure for the proposal for a law of the Socialist Parliamentary Group, presented in January in this House, on historical and democratic memory, and to which the content of the initiative is undoubtedly to be added, since, in this, although not formally, but materially, self-amendments are announced to that other initiative of updating the Historical Memory Act, which is still - and I will say in a cordial way - a picturesque parliamentary method, because it is proposed, by two parliamentary groups, to urge the Government - although surely what is intended is something else - to adopt a series of measures affecting a legislative initiative presented by one of them, and still pending processing; this is not going to happen, and not just for a reason of parliamentary law logic, but also for a political logic. The banks would have to be facilitators of ICO credits, which, if I am not mistaken, 80% would be guaranteed by the state's endorsement and 20% by the banks themselves; of those which, I remember, took EUR 60.6 billion out of everyone's pocket, and which they do not intend to recover and which, moreover, no one seems to be going to ask them to return. For those same banks, they seem to be saying that they do not want to lend that 20% endorsement, because they consider that the Spanish state is not reliable. That, I have been able to hear, and surely you will be as right as the mails that Mr Capdevila displayed, because, yesterday, a radio of a national character echoed this and provided data. Remedy this kind of barbarity, because that only gives reason to those - whom we will not stop doing, even if this data did not exist - who say that a public bank is necessary, because we do not trust private banking.
